raises the mind–brain problem and the question of how conscious and unconscious processing differ. Experiments with masking, binocular rivalry, and unattended stimuli test which signals reach awareness."},{"type":"paragraph","text":"The module also examines attention and ways to infer consciousness in unresponsive people. Pay attention to the difference between experiencing a stimulus and processing it without awareness."},{"type":"heading","id":"bp14-m13-2-study-1","text":"The central problem of consciousness"},{"type":"paragraph","text":"Consciousness refers here to awareness and experience rather than simply being awake. A person may process some information without reporting awareness of it. The relationship between physical brain events and subjective experience is part of the mind–brain problem. Monistic approaches seek to account for mental states within a physical understanding of the organism, but philosophical labels alone do not identify the neural mechanisms of awareness. Research therefore studies measurable contrasts between information that is consciously noticed and information that is not."},{"type":"heading","id":"bp14-m13-2-study-2","text":"Masking, rivalry, and unattended stimuli"},{"type":"paragraph","text":"Visual masking and binocular rivalry are examples of methods used to separate a stimulus reaching the eyes from whether it enters awareness. A stimulus can influence performance or brain responses even when a participant does not consciously report seeing it. Attention and consciousness are closely related but not identical; attention can change what becomes reportable, and unattended information may still be processed. Good experiments distinguish the physical input from the subjective report and the behavioral task."},{"type":"heading","id":"bp14-m13-2-study-3","text":"Timing and brain activity"},{"type":"paragraph","text":"Researchers compare the timing of neural events, decisions, and subjective awareness to infer how conscious processing unfolds. Brain measures may predict a response before someone can describe deciding, but interpreting this requires care about task design and how awareness was measured. Different cortical and subcortical systems participate in attention and conscious report. No single measurement has yet provided a complete explanation of subjective experience."},{"type":"heading","id":"bp14-m13-2-study-4","text":"Spatial neglect and clinical evidence"},{"type":"paragraph","text":"Brain injury can produce spatial neglect, in which a person fails to attend to or respond to parts of space despite the absence of ordinary blindness in the neglected area. Such cases reveal how awareness and attention can depend on specific networks. They also demonstrate that failure to report a stimulus is not equivalent to proving that every stage of processing was absent. Nearly all neuroscientists and philosophers accept some version of monism, the idea that mental activity is inseparable from brain activity.","paragraphIndex":20072,"bullets":[{"label":"The hard problem","explanation":"The hard problem is the question of why consciousness exists at all. Modern methods of measuring brain activity have made research on consciousness possible and respectable. Researchers present the same stimulus under conditions when an observer probably will or probably will not identify it consciously.","paragraphIndex":20073,"printedPages":[450]},{"label":"Important detail","explanation":"When someone is conscious of a stimulus, the representation of that stimulus spreads over much of the brain and rebounds back to magnify and prolong processing in the primary sensory cortex.","paragraphIndex":20074,"printedPages":[450]}],"status":"whole_book_source_structured_classroom_notes","basis":"uploaded_textbook_pdf_and_markdown","evidenceMethod":"textbook_module_summary","sourceFactCount":4,"editorialVerification":"source-aligned, individual paraphrase review pending","studyDepth":"concise_existing","editorialLesson":{"text":"Consciousness research asks about both particular experiences and the overall capacity for experience. Masking, rivalry, clinical disorders, and brain recordings address different parts of that problem. Progress depends on distinguishing what the participant sees from what they attend to, remember, decide, or can physically report.","paragraphIndex":803613,"kind":"individually_written_classroom_explanation","level":"contextual_clarification","sourceHeadingId":"bp14-m13-2-h14","sourcePrintedPage":450,"sourcePdfPage":468,"editorialStatus":"individual_topic_specific_explanation_drafted_from_source_topic_map","evidenceBasis":"Supplied Kalat 14e PDF and matching Markdown; existing source-extracted paragraphs kept as separate verifiable references","needsIndependentReview":true}}],"classroomParagraphs":["The philosopher René Descartes defended dualism, the belief in separate mind and body, but he recognized the issue of how a non-material mind could influence a physical brain.","so different from brain activity that most people take it for granted that mind and brain must be different.","In other words, we don’t know whether brain activity causes thoughts or whether thoughts cause brain activity.","But if thoughts and brain activity are the same thing, the question doesn’t make sense. Most of us find it hard to believe that the mind is a figment of our imagination.","Rene Descartes said that the only thing he could not doubt was “I think, therefore I am.” Several other psychologists have argued that consciousness exists, but we can ignore it, because it does not help us explain behavior.","However, to call consciousness a fundamental property would mean only that we have given up on explaining it, and it is certainly too soon for that.","One possibility is consciousness evolved as an “add-on” feature of brain functioning that evolved to improve our ability to survive and reproduce. To say that consciousness emerges necessarily from brain activity means that the universe is set up in such a way that certain types of neuronal activity automatically become conscious.","According to the identity position, consciousness does not explain any behavior that brain activity does not explain, because consciousness is the same thing as brain activity.","As one article insightfully pointed out, brain mapping tells us why lemon smells similar to orange, but not why lemon smells like lemon.","The best we can do is answer questions like what brain activity is necessary for consciousness.","Because that internal experience is unobservable, researchers rely on this operational definition: Someone who reports awareness of one stimulus and not another was conscious of the first and not the second. It just means that we seldom use them in research on consciousness, and if we do, we need to find a way for them to indicate awareness or unawareness of a stimulus.","Through most of the twentieth century, psychological researchers avoided the topic of consciousness altogether.","Modern methods have made consciousness research respectable because researchers can record brain activity while using procedures that permit or prevent conscious perception of a stimulus. The strong response to the flashing dots decreases awareness of the steady dot, as well as the brain’s response to it.","Many studies use masking: A brief visual stimulus is preceded, followed, or both by longer interfering stimuli.","Stanislas Dehaene and colleagues flashed a word on a screen for just 29 milliseconds (ms).","On other trials, the researchers flashed a word for the same 29 ms but preceded and followed it with masking patterns: Using fMRI and evoked potentials, the researchers found that the stimulus initially activated the primary visual cortex in both the conscious and unconscious conditions.","In the unconscious condition (masking), the stimulus spread only weakly to areas beyond the primary visual cortex, and nothing reflected it back to magnify the initial response.","Overall, the data imply that consciousness of a stimulus depends on the amount and spread of brain activity. Becoming conscious of something means that its information takes over more of your brain’s activity, and rebounds to magnify the initial activity.","Seeing something requires perceiving where it is, and the red vertical stripes cannot be in the same place as the green horizontal stripes.","Because your brain cannot perceive both patterns in the same location, your perception alternates between the two.","For most people, each perception lasts about 2 to 5 seconds before the other replaces it, although some people switch faster or slower.","These shifts, demonstrating binocular rivalry, are gradual, sweeping from one side to another. Instead of lines, the stimuli could be other images, such as a house versus a face.","Also, an emotionally charged image, such as a face with an emotional expression, holds attention longer than a neutral image.","To make the brain responses easier to distinguish, researchers presented to one eye a stationary stimulus and to the other eye a pattern that pulsated several times per second in size and brightness. When people reported consciousness of the stationary stimulus, the pulsating brain activity was weak.","A related study found that when someone switched between one perception and the other, the brain response changed first in the occipital cortex, and then spread to the other areas.","During binocular rivalry, while you are attending to the stimulus in one eye, your brain does not completely discard information from the other eye.","If it is a word from your own language, it captures your attention faster than a word from a language you do not understand. of faces and other social stimuli than nonsocial stimuli, and more rapidly become aware of a signal previously associated with danger than a safe stimulus.","Given that you become aware of a highly meaningful item faster than something less meaningful, evidently your brain must have decided that the stimulus was meaningful before you became conscious of it!","Consciousness is associated with vigorous activity in the cerebral cortex.","People who have had surgical removal of the cerebellum experience no loss of consciousness, even though the cerebellum has far more neurons and synapses than the cortex.","Evidently consciousness does not depend on the total number of neurons. During an epileptic seizure, neuronal activity is high, but it lacks informative patterns, and consciousness ceases.","Marcello Massimini and Giulio Tononi explored the key differences between conscious and unconscious activity.","If your brain had no task other than detecting the presence of light, or detecting sound waves, consciousness would be irrelevant. The cerebral cortex, unlike the cerebellum, has long-range reciprocal connections enabling each area to influence many others and receive feedback from the others.","Consider the phi phenomenon that perceptual researchers noted long ago: In the simplest case, you see a dot alternating between one position and another, side by side.","It appears to move between the two positions, even though you never see it in the space between them.","When you saw it in the first position, you didn’t know it was going to appear in the second position.","Evidently, you perceived it as moving from one position to the second after it appeared in the second position! In other words, the second position changed your perception of what occurred before it.","Another example: Suppose you hear a recorded word that is carefully engineered to sound halfway between dent and tent.","If you hear it in the phrase “*ent in the fender,” it sounds like dent.","Sometimes the pattern appears on the left of the screen and sometimes on the right. The stimulus is adjusted so you are correct a little more than chance, but you often say you didn’t see it at all.","Also, consciousness depends on reciprocal connections that compare and integrate several types of information.","Neuroscience asks two related but distinct questions: what makes a particular stimulus conscious to an alert person, and what makes a person capable of conscious experience at all. The second question is difficult because consciousness is not always identical to an observable response.","Earlier sections examine the difference between processing a stimulus and consciously noticing it. This concerns awareness of particular information within an otherwise awake person.","The broader question concerns whether a person can experience anything, as during comparisons between wakefulness and anesthesia. Researchers compare brain activity across such states.","This is not an altogether easy question for researchers, because we are not always certain whether someone is conscious.","People with blindsight respond to a visual stimulus while insisting that they did not see it.","When people are forced to awaken suddenly during anesthesia, they do not always report that they had been unconscious.","Studies have followed people as they lost consciousness under anesthesia and then regained it as the drug Loss of consciousness was marked by decreased overall activity, decreased dopamine input to parts of the cortex, and decreased connectivity between the thalamus and cortex.","Recovery of consciousness depended on increased connectivity between subcortical and cortical areas, and then by increased activity in the cortex.","Recall the earlier discussion that consciousness of a stimulus requires a spread of activity over much of the brain.","With a loss of connectivity, no stimulus can spread its activity, and consciousness ceases. Researchers used brief magnetic stimulation to activate a localized brain area, and then used EEG to observe the spread of activity.","This method offers a potentially quick way to probe for consciousness in an unresponsive person.","The extreme position is solipsism, the belief that “I alone am conscious.” (Solipsists never organize","Every solipsist is convinced that all the other solipsists are wrong.) For those of us who are not solipsists, the question is to what other species, if any, shall we attribute consciousness.","Given the neurological similarities between humans and other species, there is no compelling reason to assume that only humans are conscious. Most people believe that their pet dog or cat is conscious, and if dogs and cats are, then probably other mammals also.","When mice go from sleep to wakefulness, their brain activity changes in about the same way that human brains change when we gain consciousness.","At least some insects and other invertebrates apparently feel pain, suggesting conscious experience. The monkeys demonstrate the same distinction between conscious and unconscious stimuli that people do.","Attention isn’t synonymous with consciousness, but it is closely related.","Of all the information that strikes your eyes, ears, and other receptors, you are conscious of only those few to which you direct your attention. Consider inattentional blindness or change blindness: If something in a complex scene changes slowly, or","changes while you blink your eyes, you probably will not notice it unless you were paying attention to the item that changed.","Top-down attention depends on parts of the prefrontal and parietal cortex that facilitate responsiveness in thalamus, which in turn modifies responses in the primary sensory cortices.","Neural responses to the attended stimuli increase and responses to other stimuli decrease.","Artificially stimulating those neurons in the frontal cortex even slightly, not enough to evoke an eye movement, increases attention to the spot where the eyes would have moved, and therefore increases the ability to notice anything that appears there. They intentionally spread out their attention, and the result was that they were reasonably accurate throughout the visual field, but less accurate than usual in the foveal region.","One of psychologists’ favorite ways to study attention is the Stroop effect, the difficulty of ignoring words and saying the color of ink.","However, when people successfully do so, they enhance the activity in the color-vision areas of the cortex and decrease the activity in the areas responsible for identifying words. Anything that is different attracts attention, and on the average, people found the circle more slowly on trials with a red square present.","If asked to point straight ahead, most patients with neglect point to the right of center.","Some patients with neglect also exhibit deviations when estimating the midpoint of a numerical range.","The correct answer is, of course, 15, but some people with neglect say “17.” Evidently, they discount the lower numbers as if they were on the left side.","Researchers presented some made-up statements about the past and the future, and then tested how well people with spatial neglect remembered the statements. Although some neglect patients have sensory losses, in many cases, the main problem is loss of attention rather than impaired sensation.","However, when she was then asked to cross off all the H’s, she crossed off only the ones on the right.","The researchers summarized by saying she saw the forest but only half the trees. For example, some patients with neglect usually report feeling nothing with the left hand, especially if the right hand feels something at the time.","Again, the conclusion is that neglect is not due to a loss of sensation but a difficulty in directing attention to the left side.","Dualism—the belief in a nonmaterial mind that exists separately from the body—conflicts with the conservation of matter and energy. Nearly all neuroscientists and philosophers accept some version of monism, the idea that mental activity is inseparable from brain activity.","The hard problem is the question of why consciousness exists at all. Modern methods of measuring brain activity have made research on consciousness possible and respectable. Researchers present the same stimulus under conditions when an observer probably will or probably will not identify it consciously.","When someone is conscious of a stimulus, the representation of that stimulus spreads over much of the brain and rebounds back to magnify and prolong processing in the primary sensory cortex.","A person may react without reporting conscious awareness, as illustrated by blindsight, and may retain some awareness despite being unable to respond. This makes it necessary to infer consciousness cautiously rather than relying on behavior alone."],"classroomResearch":{"title":"Evidence in context: Experiments Using Masking","body":"Using fMRI and Compare factual decisions, value decisions, and social responses without assuming the same mechanism explains all three."},{"type":"heading","id":"bp14-m13-3-study-1","text":"Evidence-based decisions"},{"type":"paragraph","text":"Deciding often involves collecting information and selecting an action when evidence becomes sufficient. Perceptual decision experiments investigate how neural populations represent possible choices and changes in confidence or accumulated evidence. These tasks help link neuronal activity to measurable responses. An observed buildup of activity can be consistent with a decision process, though researchers must consider alternative interpretations involving movement preparation or attention."},{"type":"heading","id":"bp14-m13-3-study-2","text":"Value decisions"},{"type":"paragraph","text":"Choices involving rewards and costs recruit mechanisms that assess expected outcomes and compare options. Decisions about value are not simply the same as deciding which visual stimulus is present. Motivation, learning, context, and prior experience influence the comparison. A neural response related to reward does not imply a single 'pleasure center' that determines every choice. Multiple systems contribute to selecting what to pursue and how strongly to pursue it."},{"type":"heading","id":"bp14-m13-3-study-3","text":"Individual differences"},{"type":"paragraph","text":"People vary in impulsivity, preferences, sensitivity to reward, and reactions to uncertainty.
